### Climate

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Climate")]

Main article: [Climate of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Alaska "Climate of Alaska")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Public_Lands_Held_by_the_National_Forest_Service_and_the_Bureau_of_Land_Management.svg/220px-Public_Lands_Held_by_the_National_Forest_Service_and_the_Bureau_of_Land_Management.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Public_Lands_Held_by_the_National_Forest_Service_and_the_Bureau_of_Land_Management.svg)

Alaska has more acreage of public land owned by the federal government than any other state.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-63)

Alaska is the coldest state in the United States.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64) The climate in the south and southeastern Alaska is a mid-latitude [oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate "Oceanic climate") ([Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification"): *Cfb*), and a subarctic oceanic climate (Köppen *Cfc*) in the northern parts, with cool summers and relatively mild winters. On an annual basis, the southeast is both the wettest and warmest part of Alaska with milder temperatures in the winter and high precipitation throughout the year. Juneau averages over 50 in (130 cm) of precipitation a year, and [Ketchikan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketchikan,_Alaska "Ketchikan, Alaska") averages over 150 in (380 cm).[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-65) This is also the only region in Alaska in which the average daytime high temperature is above freezing during the winter months.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_USA_Alaska_1991%E2%80%932020.svg/220px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_USA_Alaska_1991%E2%80%932020.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_USA_Alaska_1991%E2%80%932020.svg)

[Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification") of Alaska

The climate of [Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchorage,_Alaska "Anchorage, Alaska") and south central Alaska is mild by Alaskan standards due to the region's proximity to the seacoast. While the area gets less rain than southeast Alaska, it gets more snow, and days tend to be clearer. On average, [Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchorage,_Alaska "Anchorage, Alaska") receives 16 in (41 cm) of precipitation a year, with around 75 in (190 cm) of snow, although there are areas in the south central which receive far more snow. It is a subarctic climate ([Köppen: *Dfc*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Group_D:_Continental/microthermal_climates "Köppen climate classification")) due to its brief, cool summers.

The climate of [western Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Alaska "Southwest Alaska") is determined in large part by the [Bering Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bering_Sea "Bering Sea") and the [Gulf of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Alaska "Gulf of Alaska"). It is a subarctic oceanic climate in the southwest and a continental subarctic climate farther north. The temperature is somewhat moderate considering how far north the area is. This region has a tremendous amount of variety in precipitation. An area stretching from the northern side of the Seward Peninsula to the [Kobuk River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kobuk_River "Kobuk River") valley (i.e., the region around [Kotzebue Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kotzebue_Sound "Kotzebue Sound")) is technically a [desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert "Desert"), with portions receiving less than 10 in (25 cm) of precipitation annually. On the other extreme, some locations between [Dillingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dillingham,_Alaska "Dillingham, Alaska") and [Bethel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethel,_Alaska "Bethel, Alaska") average around 100 in (250 cm) of precipitation.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AK-YK_Precip-66)

The climate of the interior of Alaska is subarctic and is a classic example of a continental subarctic climate, except in a few valleys where the climate approaches [humid continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate "Humid continental climate") (Köppen: *Dfb*). Some of the highest and lowest temperatures in Alaska occur around the area near [Fairbanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairbanks,_Alaska "Fairbanks, Alaska"). Summers are warm (albeit generally short) and may have temperatures reaching into the 90s °F (the low-to-mid 30s °C), while in the long and very cold winters, the temperature can fall below −60 °F (−51 °C). Precipitation is sparse in the Interior, often less than 10 in (25 cm) a year, but what precipitation falls in the winter tends to stay the entire winter.

The highest and lowest recorded temperatures in Alaska are both in the Interior. The highest is 100 °F (38 °C) in [Fort Yukon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Yukon,_Alaska "Fort Yukon, Alaska") (which is just 8 mi or 13 km inside the arctic circle) on June 27, 1915,[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NOAA-All_Hazards-67)[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-DRI-68) making Alaska tied with Hawaii as the state with the lowest high temperature in the United States.[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-69)[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-70) The lowest official Alaska temperature is −80 °F (−62 °C) in [Prospect Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prospect_Creek,_Alaska "Prospect Creek, Alaska") on January 23, 1971,[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NOAA-All_Hazards-67)[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-DRI-68) one degree above the lowest temperature recorded in continental North America (in [Snag, Yukon, Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snag,_Yukon "Snag, Yukon")).[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-71)

The climate in the extreme north of Alaska, north of the [Brooks Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooks_Range "Brooks Range"), is [Arctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_climate "Polar climate") ([Köppen: *ET*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Group_E:_Polar_climates "Köppen climate classification")) with long, very cold winters and short, cool summers. Even in July, the average low temperature in [Utqiaġvik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utqia%C4%A1vik,_Alaska "Utqiaġvik, Alaska") is 34 °F (1 °C).[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72) Precipitation is light in this part of Alaska, with many places averaging less than 10 in (25 cm) per year, mostly as snow which stays on the ground almost the entire year.

The [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau "United States Census Bureau") found in the [2020 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census "2020 United States census") that the population of Alaska was 733,391 on April 1, 2020, a 3.3% increase since the [2010 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census "2010 United States census").[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-2020Census-6) According to the 2010 United States Census, the U.S. state of Alaska had a population of 710,231, a 13.3% increase from 626,932 at the 2000 U.S. census.

In 2020, Alaska ranked as the 48th largest state by population, ahead of only [Vermont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vermont "Vermont") and [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming "Wyoming").[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-76) Alaska is the least densely populated state, and one of the most sparsely populated areas in the world, at 1.2 inhabitants per square mile (0.46/km2), with the next state, Wyoming, at 5.8 inhabitants per square mile (2.2/km2).[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77) Alaska is by far the largest U.S. state by [area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area "List of U.S. states and territories by area"), and the tenth wealthiest (per capita income).[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78) As of 2018[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit) due to its population size, it is one of 14 U.S. states that still have only one telephone [area code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbering_plan "Telephone numbering plan").[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-US_area_codes-79)

According to [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development "United States Department of Housing and Urban Development")'s 2022 [Annual Homeless Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress "Annual Homeless Assessment Report to Congress"), there were an estimated 2,320 [homeless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness "Homelessness") people in Alaska.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-80)[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Ethnic_Origins_in_Alaska.png/270px-Ethnic_Origins_in_Alaska.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ethnic_Origins_in_Alaska.png)

Ethnic origins in Alaska

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Alaska_racial_and_ethnic_map.svg/220px-Alaska_racial_and_ethnic_map.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alaska_racial_and_ethnic_map.svg)

Map of the largest racial/ethnic group by borough. Red indicates Native American, blue indicates non-Hispanic white, and green indicates Asian. Darker shades indicate a higher proportion of the population.

The 2019 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey "American Community Survey") estimated 60.2% of the population was [non-Hispanic white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_whites "Non-Hispanic whites"), 3.7% [black or African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans "African Americans"), 15.6% [American Indian or Alaska Native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States "Native Americans in the United States"), 6.5% [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans "Asian Americans"), 1.4% [Native Hawaiian and other Pacific Islander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander_Americans "Pacific Islander Americans"), 7.5% two or more races, and 7.3% [Hispanic or Latin American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans "Hispanic and Latino Americans") of any race. At the survey estimates, 7.8% of the total population was foreign-born from 2015 to 2019.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86) In 2015, 61.3% was non-Hispanic white, 3.4% black or African American, 13.3% American Indian or Alaska Native, 6.2% Asian, 0.9% Native Hawaiian and other Pacific Islander, 0.3% some other race, and 7.7% multiracial. Hispanics and Latin Americans were 7% of the state population in 2015.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-87) From 2015 to 2019, the largest Hispanic and Latin American groups were [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans "Mexican Americans"), [Puerto Ricans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Ricans "Puerto Ricans"), and [Cuban Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Americans "Cuban Americans"). The largest Asian groups living in the state were [Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Americans "Filipino Americans"), [Korean Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Americans "Korean Americans"), and [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Americans "Japanese Americans") and [Chinese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Americans "Chinese Americans").[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88)

The state was 66.7% white (64.1% non-Hispanic white), 14.8% American Indian and Alaska Native, 5.4% Asian, 3.3% black or African American, 1.0% Native Hawaiian and other Pacific Islander, 1.6% from some other race, and 7.3% from two or more races in 2010. Hispanics or Latin Americans of any race made up 5.5% of the population in 2010.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89) As of 2011[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit), 50.7% of Alaska's population younger than one year of age belonged to minority groups (i.e., did not have two parents of non-Hispanic white ancestry).[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-90) In 1960, the United States Census Bureau reported Alaska's population as 77.2% white, 3% black, and 18.8% American Indian and Alaska Native.[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91)

In 2018, the top countries of origin for Alaska's immigrants were the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines"), [Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico "Mexico"), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada "Canada"), [Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand "Thailand") and [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korea "South Korea").[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92)

### Languages

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=25 "Edit section: Languages")]

Further information: [Alaska Native languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_languages "Alaska Native languages")

According to the 2011 American Community Survey, 83.4% of people over the age of five spoke only English at home. About 3.5% spoke Spanish at home, 2.2% spoke another [Indo-European language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages "Indo-European languages"), about 4.3% spoke an [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Asia "Languages of Asia") language (including [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language "Tagalog language")),[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93) and about 5.3% spoke other languages at home.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94) In 2019, the American Community Survey determined 83.7% spoke only English, and 16.3% spoke another language other than English. The most spoken European language after English was Spanish, spoken by approximately 4.0% of the state population. Collectively, Asian and Pacific Islander languages were spoken by 5.6% of Alaskans.[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95) Since 2010, a total of 5.2% of Alaskans speak one of the state's 20 [indigenous languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_languages_of_the_Americas "Indigenous languages of the Americas"),[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-96) known locally as "native languages".

The [Alaska Native Language Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_Language_Center "Alaska Native Language Center") at the [University of Alaska Fairbanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alaska_Fairbanks "University of Alaska Fairbanks") claims that at least 20 [Alaskan native languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_languages "Alaska Native languages") exist and there are also some languages with different dialects.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-uaf.edu-97) Most of Alaska's native languages belong to either the [Eskimo–Aleut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eskimo%E2%80%93Aleut_languages "Eskimo–Aleut languages") or [Na-Dene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Na-Dene_languages "Na-Dene languages") language families; some languages are thought to be [isolates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_isolate "Language isolate") (e.g. [Haida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haida_language "Haida language")) or have not yet been classified (e.g. [Tsimshianic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsimshianic_languages "Tsimshianic languages")).[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-uaf.edu-97) As of 2014[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit) nearly all of Alaska's native languages were classified as either threatened, shifting, moribund, nearly extinct, or dormant languages.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-98)

In October 2014, the governor of Alaska signed a bill declaring the state's 20 indigenous languages to have official status.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99)[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100) This bill gave them symbolic recognition as official languages, though they have not been adopted for official use within the government. The 20 languages that were included in the bill are:

1. [Inupiaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inupiat_language "Inupiat language")
2. [Siberian Yupik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Siberian_Yupik_language "Central Siberian Yupik language")
3. [Central Alaskan Yup'ik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Alaskan_Yup%27ik_language "Central Alaskan Yup'ik language")
4. [Alutiiq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alutiiq_language "Alutiiq language")
5. [Unangax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleut_language "Aleut language")
6. [Dena'ina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dena%27ina_language "Dena'ina language")
7. [Deg Xinag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deg_Xinag_language "Deg Xinag language")
8. [Holikachuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holikachuk_language "Holikachuk language")
9. [Koyukon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koyukon_language "Koyukon language")
10. [Upper Kuskokwim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Kuskokwim_language "Upper Kuskokwim language")
11. [Gwich'in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwich%27in_language "Gwich'in language")
12. [Tanana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Tanana_language "Lower Tanana language")
13. [Upper Tanana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Tanana_language "Upper Tanana language")
14. [Tanacross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanacross_language "Tanacross language")
15. [Hän](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A4n_language "Hän language")
16. [Ahtna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahtna_language "Ahtna language")
17. [Eyak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyak_language "Eyak language")
18. [Tlingit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tlingit_language "Tlingit language")
19. [Haida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haida_language "Haida language")
20. [Tsimshian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coast_Tsimshian_dialect "Coast Tsimshian dialect")

### Religion

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=26 "Edit section: Religion")]

See also: [Alaska Native religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_religion "Alaska Native religion") and [Shamanism among Alaska Natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamanism_among_Alaska_Natives "Shamanism among Alaska Natives")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Sitka_-_St._Michael%27s_Russian_Orthodox_Cathedral.jpg/183px-Sitka_-_St._Michael%27s_Russian_Orthodox_Cathedral.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sitka_-_St._Michael%27s_Russian_Orthodox_Cathedral.jpg)

[St. Michael's Russian Orthodox Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Michael%27s_Cathedral_\(Sitka,_Alaska\) "St. Michael's Cathedral (Sitka, Alaska)") in downtown [Sitka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitka,_Alaska "Sitka, Alaska")

Religious self-identification in Alaska per the [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute "Public Religion Research Institute")'s 2020 survey[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101)

  [Unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_the_United_States "Irreligion in the United States") (37%)

  [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_the_United_States "Protestantism in the United States") (36%)

  [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism_in_the_United_States "Catholicism in the United States") (14%)

  [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_the_United_States "Eastern Orthodoxy in the United States") (4%)

  [Mormonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormonism_in_the_United_States "Mormonism in the United States") (2%)

  [Jehovah's Witness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witness "Jehovah's Witness") (1%)

  Other (6%)

Multiple surveys have ranked Alaska among [the most irreligious states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_religiosity "List of U.S. states and territories by religiosity").[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102)[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-103)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/ChangePoint_building.jpg/150px-ChangePoint_building.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ChangePoint_building.jpg)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Anchorage_Baptist_Temple.jpg/150px-Anchorage_Baptist_Temple.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Anchorage_Baptist_Temple.jpg)

ChangePoint in south Anchorage (left) and Anchorage Baptist Temple in east Anchorage (right) are Alaska's largest churches in terms of attendance and membership.

According to statistics collected by the [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives "Association of Religion Data Archives") (ARDA) from 2010, about 34% of Alaska residents were members of religious congregations. Of the religious population, roughly 4% were Mormon, 0.5% [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism "Judaism"), 0.5% Muslim, 1% Buddhist, 0.2% Baháʼí, and 0.5% Hindu.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-104) The largest religious denominations in Alaska as of 2010[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit) was the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church") with 50,866 adherents; non-denominational Evangelicals with 38,070 adherents; [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints "The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints") with 32,170 adherents; and the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention "Southern Baptist Convention") with 19,891 adherents.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-www.thearda.com-105)[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-106) Alaska has been identified, along with Washington and Oregon in the Pacific Northwest, as being [the least religious states in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unchurched_Belt "Unchurched Belt"), in terms of church membership.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-107)[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-108)

The Pew Research Center in 2014 determined 62% of the adult population practiced Christianity. Of the Christian denominations, Catholicism was the largest Christian group. When Protestant denominations were combined, Protestantism was the largest Christian tradition, with Evangelicalism being the largest movement within the Protestant group. The unaffiliated population made up the largest non-Christian religious affiliation at 37%. [Atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism "Atheism") made up 5% of the population and the largest non-Christian religion was [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism"). In 2020, the Public Religion Research Institute (PRRI) determined 57% of adults were Christian.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-109) By 2022, Christianity increased to 77% of the population according to the PRRI.

Through the Association of Religion Data Archives in 2020, its Christian population was dominated by [non/inter-denominational Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity "Nondenominational Christianity") as the single largest Christian cohort, with 73,930 adherents. Roman Catholics were second with 40,280 members; throughout its Christian population, non-denominational Christians had an adherence rate of 100.81 per 1,000 residents, and Catholics 54.92 per 1,000 residents.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:1-110) Per 2014's Pew study, religion was seen as very important to 41% of the population, although 29% considered it somewhat important.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-111) In 2014, Pew determined roughly 55% believed in God with absolute certainty, and 24% believed fairly certainly. Reflecting the separate 2020 ARDA study, the 2014 Pew study showed 30% attended religious services once a week, 34% once or twice a month, and 36% seldom/never.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-111) In 2018, *[The Gospel Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gospel_Coalition "The Gospel Coalition")* published an article using Pew data and determined non-churchgoing Christians nationwide did not attend religious services often through the following: practicing the faith in other ways, not finding a house of worship they liked, disliking sermons and feeling unwelcomed, and logistics.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112)

In 1795, the first [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church "Russian Orthodox Church") was established in [Kodiak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kodiak_Island_Borough,_Alaska "Kodiak Island Borough, Alaska"). Intermarriage with Alaskan Natives helped the Russian immigrants integrate into society. As a result, an increasing number of Russian Orthodox churches gradually became established within Alaska.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-113) Alaska also has the largest [Quaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaker "Quaker") population (by percentage) of any state.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-114) In 2009, there were 6,000 Jews in Alaska (for whom observance of [halakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha "Halakha") [may pose special problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_law_in_the_polar_regions "Jewish law in the polar regions")).[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-115) Alaskan Hindus often share venues and celebrations with members of other Asian religious communities, including [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism "Sikhism") and [Jains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism "Jainism").[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-116)[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-117)[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-118) In 2010, Alaskan Hindus established the [Sri Ganesha Temple of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Ganesha_Temple_of_Alaska "Sri Ganesha Temple of Alaska"), making it the first Hindu Temple in Alaska and the northernmost Hindu Temple in the world. There are an estimated 2,000–3,000 Hindus in Alaska. The vast majority of Hindus live in Anchorage or Fairbanks.

Estimates for the number of Muslims in Alaska range from 2,000 to 5,000.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-119)[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-120)[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-121) In 2020, ARDA estimated there were 400 Muslims in the state.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:1-110) The [Islamic Community Center of Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Community_Center_of_Anchorage,_Alaska "Islamic Community Center of Anchorage, Alaska") began efforts in the late 1990s to construct a mosque in Anchorage. They broke ground on a building in south Anchorage in 2010 and were nearing completion in late 2014. When completed, the mosque was the first in the state and one of the northernmost mosques in the world.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-122) There is also a [Baháʼí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith "Baháʼí Faith") center,[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-123) and there were 690 adherents in 2020.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:1-110) Additionally, there were 469 adherents of [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_the_United_States "Hinduism in the United States") and [Yoga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoga "Yoga") altogether in 2020, and a small number of [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_United_States "Buddhism in the United States") were present.

## Economy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=27 "Edit section: Economy")]

Main article: [Economy of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Alaska "Economy of Alaska")

See also: [Alaska locations by per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_locations_by_per_capita_income "Alaska locations by per capita income") and [List of Alaska companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Alaska_companies "List of Alaska companies")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Prudhoe_Bay_aerial_FWS.jpg/220px-Prudhoe_Bay_aerial_FWS.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Prudhoe_Bay_aerial_FWS.jpg)

Aerial view of infrastructure at the [Prudhoe Bay Oil Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prudhoe_Bay_Oil_Field "Prudhoe Bay Oil Field")

As of October 2022, Alaska had a total employment of 316,900. The number of employer establishments was 21,077.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-124)

The 2018 [gross state product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_state_product "Gross state product") was $55 billion, 48th in the U.S. Its [per capita personal income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_GDP_per_capita_\(nominal\) "List of U.S. states by GDP per capita (nominal)") for 2018 was $73,000, ranking 7th in the nation. According to a 2013 study by Phoenix Marketing International, Alaska had the fifth-largest number of millionaires per capita in the United States, with a ratio of 6.75 percent.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-125) The oil and gas industry dominates the Alaskan economy, with more than 80% of the state's revenues derived from petroleum extraction. Alaska's main export product (excluding oil and natural gas) is seafood, primarily salmon, cod, pollock and crab.

Agriculture represents a very small fraction of the Alaskan economy. Agricultural production is primarily for consumption within the state and includes nursery stock, dairy products, vegetables, and livestock. Manufacturing is limited, with most foodstuffs and general goods imported from elsewhere.

Employment is primarily in government and industries such as natural resource extraction, shipping, and transportation. Military bases are a significant component of the economy in the Fairbanks North Star, Anchorage and Kodiak Island boroughs, as well as Kodiak. Federal subsidies are also an important part of the economy, allowing the state to keep taxes low. Its industrial outputs are crude petroleum, natural gas, coal, [gold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold "Gold"), [precious metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precious_metals "Precious metals"), [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc "Zinc") and other mining, seafood processing, timber and wood products. There is also a growing service and tourism sector. Tourists have contributed to the economy by supporting local lodging.

### Energy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=28 "Edit section: Energy")]

See also: [Natural gas in Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_in_Alaska "Natural gas in Alaska"), [List of power stations in Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_power_stations_in_Alaska "List of power stations in Alaska"), and [Energy law § Alaska law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_law#Alaska_law "Energy law")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Trans-Alaska_Pipeline_System_Luca_Galuzzi_2005.jpg/220px-Trans-Alaska_Pipeline_System_Luca_Galuzzi_2005.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Trans-Alaska_Pipeline_System_Luca_Galuzzi_2005.jpg)

The [Trans-Alaska Pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Alaska_Pipeline "Trans-Alaska Pipeline") transports oil, Alaska's most financially important export, from the [North Slope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_North_Slope "Alaska North Slope") to [Valdez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valdez,_Alaska "Valdez, Alaska"). The [heat pipes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_pipe "Heat pipe") in the column mounts are pertinent, since they disperse heat upwards and prevent melting of [permafrost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permafrost "Permafrost").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Alaska_Crude_Oil_Reserves.PNG/170px-Alaska_Crude_Oil_Reserves.PNG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alaska_Crude_Oil_Reserves.PNG)

Alaska [proven oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proven_reserves "Proven reserves") peaked in 1973 and have declined more than 60% since then.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Alaska_crude_oil_production_in_1970_through_2020_%2851140868890%29.png/220px-Alaska_crude_oil_production_in_1970_through_2020_%2851140868890%29.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alaska_crude_oil_production_in_1970_through_2020_\(51140868890\).png)

Alaskan oil production peaked in 1988 and has declined more than 75% since then.

Alaska has vast energy resources, although its oil reserves have been largely depleted. Major oil and gas reserves were found in the [Alaska North Slope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_North_Slope "Alaska North Slope") (ANS) and Cook Inlet basins, but according to the [Energy Information Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_Information_Administration "Energy Information Administration"), by February 2014 Alaska had fallen to fourth place in the nation in crude oil production after Texas, [North Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Dakota "North Dakota"), and [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California "California").[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-126)[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-127) Prudhoe Bay on Alaska's North Slope is still the second highest-yielding oil field in the United States, typically producing about 400,000 barrels per day (64,000 m3/d), although by early 2014 North Dakota's [Bakken Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakken_Formation "Bakken Formation") was producing over 900,000 barrels per day (140,000 m3/d).[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-128) Prudhoe Bay was the largest [conventional oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conventional_oil "Conventional oil") field ever discovered in North America, but was much smaller than Canada's enormous [Athabasca oil sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca_oil_sands "Athabasca oil sands") field, which by 2014 was producing about 1,500,000 barrels per day (240,000 m3/d) of [unconventional oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unconventional_oil "Unconventional oil"), and had hundreds of years of producible reserves at that rate.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-129)

The [Trans-Alaska Pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Alaska_Pipeline "Trans-Alaska Pipeline") can transport and pump up to 2.1 million barrels (330,000 m3) of crude oil per day, more than any other crude oil pipeline in the United States. Additionally, substantial coal deposits are found in Alaska's bituminous, sub-bituminous, and lignite coal basins. The [United States Geological Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Geological_Survey "United States Geological Survey") estimates that there are 85.4 trillion cubic feet (2,420 km3) of undiscovered, technically recoverable gas from natural gas hydrates on the Alaskan North Slope.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-130) Alaska also offers some of the highest hydroelectric power potential in the country from its numerous rivers. Large swaths of the Alaskan coastline offer wind and geothermal energy potential as well.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-131)

Alaska's economy depends heavily on increasingly expensive diesel fuel for heating, transportation, electric power and light. Although wind and hydroelectric power are abundant and underdeveloped, proposals for statewide energy systems were judged uneconomical (at the time of the report, 2001) due to low (less than 50¢/gal) fuel prices, long distances and low population.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-132) The cost of a gallon of gas in urban Alaska is usually thirty to sixty cents higher than the national average; prices in rural areas are generally significantly higher but vary widely depending on transportation costs, seasonal usage peaks, nearby petroleum development infrastructure and many other factors.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-133)[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-134)

#### Permanent Fund

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Permanent Fund")]

The [Alaska Permanent Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Permanent_Fund "Alaska Permanent Fund") is a constitutionally authorized appropriation of oil revenues, established by voters in 1976 to manage a surplus in state petroleum revenues from oil, largely in anticipation of the then recently constructed [Trans-Alaska Pipeline System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Alaska_Pipeline_System "Trans-Alaska Pipeline System"). The fund was originally proposed by Governor [Keith Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_Harvey_Miller "Keith Harvey Miller") on the eve of the 1969 Prudhoe Bay lease sale, out of fear that the legislature would spend the entire proceeds of the sale (which amounted to $900 million) at once. It was later championed by Governor [Jay Hammond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Hammond "Jay Hammond") and [Kenai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenai,_Alaska "Kenai, Alaska") [state representative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_House_of_Representatives "Alaska House of Representatives") Hugh Malone. It has served as an attractive political prospect ever since, diverting revenues which would normally be deposited into the general fund.

The [Alaska Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Constitution "Alaska Constitution") was written so as to discourage dedicating state funds for a particular purpose. The Permanent Fund has become the rare exception to this, mostly due to the political climate of distrust existing during the time of its creation. From its initial principal of $734,000, the fund has grown to $50 billion as a result of oil royalties and capital investment programs.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-135) Most if not all the principal is invested conservatively outside Alaska. This has led to frequent calls by Alaskan politicians for the Fund to make investments within Alaska, though such a stance has never gained momentum.

Starting in 1982, dividends from the fund's annual growth have been paid out each year to eligible Alaskans, ranging from an initial $1,000 in 1982 (equal to three years' payout, as the distribution of payments was held up in a lawsuit over the distribution scheme) to $3,269 in 2008 (which included a one-time $1,200 "Resource Rebate"). Every year, the state legislature takes out 8% from the earnings, puts 3% back into the principal for inflation proofing, and the remaining 5% is distributed to all qualifying Alaskans. To qualify for the Permanent Fund Dividend, one must have lived in the state for a minimum of 12 months, maintain constant residency subject to allowable absences,[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-136) and not be subject to court judgments or criminal convictions which fall under various disqualifying classifications or may subject the payment amount to civil garnishment.

The Permanent Fund is often considered to be one of the leading examples of a [basic income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_income "Basic income") policy in the world.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-137)

### Cost of living

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Cost of living")]

The cost of goods in Alaska has long been higher than in the contiguous 48 states. Federal government employees, particularly [United States Postal Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postal_Service "United States Postal Service") (USPS) workers and active-duty military members, receive a Cost of Living Allowance usually set at 25% of base pay because, while the cost of living has gone down, it is still one of the highest in the country.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:1a-138)

Rural Alaska suffers from extremely high prices for food and consumer goods compared to the rest of the country, due to the relatively limited transportation infrastructure.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:1a-138)

### Agriculture and fishing

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=31 "Edit section: Agriculture and fishing")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Pacific_Halibut_Fileting.JPG/170px-Pacific_Halibut_Fileting.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pacific_Halibut_Fileting.JPG)

[Halibut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halibut "Halibut"), both as a sport fish and commercially, is important to the state's economy.

Due to the northern climate and short growing season, relatively little farming occurs in Alaska. Most farms are in either the [Matanuska Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matanuska_Valley "Matanuska Valley"), about 40 miles (64 km) northeast of [Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchorage "Anchorage"), or on the [Kenai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenai_Peninsula "Kenai Peninsula"), about 60 miles (97 km) southwest of Anchorage. The short 100-day growing season limits the crops that can be grown, but the long sunny summer days make for productive growing seasons. The primary crops are potatoes, carrots, lettuce, and cabbage.

The [Tanana Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanana_Valley "Tanana Valley") is another notable agricultural locus, especially the [Delta Junction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_Junction,_Alaska "Delta Junction, Alaska") area, about 100 miles (160 km) southeast of Fairbanks, with a sizable concentration of farms growing agronomic crops; these farms mostly lie north and east of [Fort Greely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Greely "Fort Greely"). This area was largely set aside and developed under a state program spearheaded by Hammond during his second term as governor. Delta-area crops consist predominantly of barley and hay. West of Fairbanks lies another concentration of small farms catering to restaurants, the hotel and tourist industry, and [community-supported agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community-supported_agriculture "Community-supported agriculture").

Alaskan agriculture has experienced a surge in growth of [market gardeners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_gardener "Market gardener"), small farms and [farmers' markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farmers%27_market "Farmers' market") in recent years, with the highest percentage increase (46%) in the nation in growth in farmers' markets in 2011, compared to 17% nationwide.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-139) The [peony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peony "Peony") industry has also taken off, as the growing season allows farmers to harvest during a gap in supply elsewhere in the world, thereby filling a niche in the flower market.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-140)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Heavy-lift_melon.jpg/200px-Heavy-lift_melon.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Heavy-lift_melon.jpg)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Monster_vegetable_display_at_the_Tanana_Valley_State_Fair_2010.jpg/200px-Monster_vegetable_display_at_the_Tanana_Valley_State_Fair_2010.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Monster_vegetable_display_at_the_Tanana_Valley_State_Fair_2010.jpg)

Oversized vegetables on display at the [Alaska State Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_State_Fair "Alaska State Fair") (left) and the [Tanana Valley State Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanana_Valley_State_Fair "Tanana Valley State Fair") (right)

Alaska, with no counties, lacks county fairs. Instead, a small assortment of state and local fairs (with the [Alaska State Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_State_Fair "Alaska State Fair") in [Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmer,_Alaska "Palmer, Alaska") the largest), are held mostly in the late summer. The fairs are mostly located in communities with historic or current agricultural activity, and feature local farmers exhibiting produce in addition to more high-profile commercial activities such as carnival rides, concerts and food. "Alaska Grown" is used as an agricultural slogan.

Alaska has an abundance of seafood, with the primary fisheries in the Bering Sea and the North Pacific. Seafood is one of the few food items that is often cheaper within the state than outside it. Many Alaskans take advantage of salmon seasons to harvest portions of their household diet while fishing for subsistence, as well as sport. This includes fish taken by hook, net or [wheel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_wheel "Fish wheel").[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-141)

Hunting for subsistence, primarily [caribou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribou "Caribou"), [moose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose "Moose"), and [Dall sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dall_sheep "Dall sheep") is still common in the state, particularly in remote [Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bush_\(Alaska\) "The Bush (Alaska)") communities. An example of a traditional native food is [Akutaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akutaq "Akutaq"), the Eskimo ice cream, which can consist of reindeer fat, seal oil, dried fish meat and local berries.

Alaska's reindeer herding is concentrated on [Seward Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seward_Peninsula "Seward Peninsula"), where wild caribou can be prevented from mingling and migrating with the domesticated reindeer.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-142)

Most food in Alaska is transported into the state from ["Outside"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outside_\(Alaska\) "Outside (Alaska)") (the other 49 U.S. states), and shipping costs make food in the cities relatively expensive. In rural areas, subsistence hunting and gathering is an essential activity because imported food is prohibitively expensive. Although most small towns and villages in Alaska lie along the coastline, the cost of importing food to remote villages can be high because of the terrain and difficult road conditions, which change dramatically due to varying climate and precipitation changes. Transport costs can reach 50¢ per pound ($1.10/kg) or higher in some remote areas during times of inclement weather or rough terrain conditions, if these locations can be reached at all. The cost of delivering a gallon (3.8 L) of milk is about $3.50 in many villages where per capita income can be $20,000 or less. Fuel cost per gallon is routinely twenty to thirty cents higher than the contiguous United States average, with only Hawaii having higher prices.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-143)[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-144)

## Culture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Culture")]

See also: [List of artists and writers from Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_artists_and_writers_from_Alaska "List of artists and writers from Alaska")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Mask_Display_at_I%C3%B1upiat_Heritage_Center.jpg/220px-Mask_Display_at_I%C3%B1upiat_Heritage_Center.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mask_Display_at_I%C3%B1upiat_Heritage_Center.jpg)

Mask Display at Iñupiat Heritage Center in [Utqiaġvik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utqia%C4%A1vik,_Alaska "Utqiaġvik, Alaska")

Some of Alaska's popular annual events are the [Iditarod Trail Sled Dog Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iditarod_Trail_Sled_Dog_Race "Iditarod Trail Sled Dog Race") from Anchorage to Nome, World Ice Art Championships in Fairbanks, the Blueberry Festival and Alaska Hummingbird Festival in [Ketchikan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketchikan,_Alaska "Ketchikan, Alaska"), the Sitka Whale Fest, and the Stikine River Garnet Fest in [Wrangell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrangell,_Alaska "Wrangell, Alaska"). The [Stikine River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stikine_River "Stikine River") attracts the largest springtime concentration of [American bald eagles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_bald_eagle "American bald eagle") in the world.

The [Alaska Native Heritage Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_Heritage_Center "Alaska Native Heritage Center") celebrates the rich heritage of Alaska's 11 cultural groups. Their purpose is to encourage cross-cultural exchanges among all people and enhance self-esteem among [Native people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas "Indigenous peoples of the Americas"). The [Alaska Native Arts Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_Arts_Foundation "Alaska Native Arts Foundation") promotes and markets Native art from all regions and cultures in the State, using the internet.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-145)

### Music

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=33 "Edit section: Music")]

Main article: [Music of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Alaska "Music of Alaska")

Influences on music in Alaska include the traditional music of Alaska Natives as well as folk music brought by later immigrants from Russia and Europe. Prominent musicians from Alaska include singer [Jewel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewel_\(singer\) "Jewel (singer)"), traditional Aleut flautist [Mary Youngblood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Youngblood "Mary Youngblood"), folk singer-songwriter [Libby Roderick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libby_Roderick "Libby Roderick"), Christian music singer-songwriter [Lincoln Brewster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Brewster "Lincoln Brewster"), metal/post hardcore band [36 Crazyfists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/36_Crazyfists "36 Crazyfists") and the groups [Pamyua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamyua "Pamyua") and [Portugal. The Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal._The_Man "Portugal. The Man").

There are many established music festivals in Alaska, including the [Alaska Folk Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Folk_Festival "Alaska Folk Festival"), the Fairbanks Summer Arts Festival, the Anchorage Folk Festival, the [Athabascan Old-Time Fiddling Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabascan_Old-Time_Fiddling_Festival "Athabascan Old-Time Fiddling Festival"), the Sitka Jazz Festival, the [Sitka Summer Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitka_Summer_Music_Festival "Sitka Summer Music Festival"), and the Anchorage Chamber Music Festival. The most prominent orchestra in Alaska is the [Anchorage Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchorage_Symphony_Orchestra "Anchorage Symphony Orchestra"), though the Fairbanks Symphony Orchestra and [Juneau Symphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneau_Symphony "Juneau Symphony") are also notable. The [Anchorage Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchorage_Opera "Anchorage Opera") is currently the state's only professional opera company, though there are several volunteer and semi-professional organizations in the state as well.

The official [state song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_songs "List of U.S. state songs") of Alaska is "[Alaska's Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska%27s_Flag "Alaska's Flag")", which was adopted in 1955; it celebrates the [flag of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Alaska "Flag of Alaska").

### Film and television

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=34 "Edit section: Film and television")]

See also: [List of films set in Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_set_in_Alaska "List of films set in Alaska")

The 1983 Disney movie *[Never Cry Wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Never_Cry_Wolf_\(film\) "Never Cry Wolf (film)")* was at least partially shot in Alaska. The 1991 film *[White Fang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Fang_\(1991_film\) "White Fang (1991 film)")*, based on [Jack London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_London "Jack London")'s 1906 novel and starring [Ethan Hawke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethan_Hawke "Ethan Hawke"), was filmed in and around [Haines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haines,_Alaska "Haines, Alaska"). [Steven Seagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Seagal "Steven Seagal")'s 1994 *[On Deadly Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_Deadly_Ground "On Deadly Ground")*, starring [Michael Caine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Caine "Michael Caine"), was filmed in part at the [Worthington Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worthington_Glacier "Worthington Glacier") near [Valdez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valdez,_Alaska "Valdez, Alaska").[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-146)

Many reality television shows are filmed in Alaska. In 2011, the *Anchorage Daily News* found ten set in the state.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hopkins20110214-147)

## Public health and safety

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=48 "Edit section: Public health and safety")]

See also: [Dentistry in rural Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dentistry_in_rural_Alaska "Dentistry in rural Alaska")

The [Alaska State Troopers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_State_Troopers "Alaska State Troopers") are Alaska's statewide police force. They have a long and storied history, but were not an official organization until 1941. Before the force was officially organized, law enforcement in Alaska was handled by various federal agencies. Larger towns usually have their own local police and some villages rely on "Public Safety Officers" who have police training but do not carry firearms. In much of the state, the troopers serve as the only police force available. In addition to enforcing traffic and criminal law, wildlife Troopers enforce hunting and fishing regulations. Due to the varied terrain and wide scope of the Troopers' duties, they employ a wide variety of land, air, and water patrol vehicles.

Many rural communities in Alaska are considered "dry", having outlawed the importation of alcoholic beverages.[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-154) Suicide rates for rural residents are higher than urban.[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-155)

[Domestic abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_abuse "Domestic abuse") and other violent crimes are also at high levels in the state; this is in part linked to alcohol abuse.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-156) Alaska has the highest rate of sexual assault in the nation, especially in rural areas. The average age of sexually assaulted victims is 16 years old. In four out of five cases, the suspects were relatives, friends, or acquaintances.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-157)

### Health insurance

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=49 "Edit section: Health insurance")]

As of 2022[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit), [CVS Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CVS_Health "CVS Health") and [Premera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premera_Blue_Cross "Premera Blue Cross") account for 47% and 46% of private health insurance, respectively.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-158) Premera and [Moda Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moda_Health "Moda Health") offer insurance on the federally-run Affordable Care Exchange.[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-159)

### Hospitals

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=50 "Edit section: Hospitals")]

Main article: [List of hospitals in Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Alaska "List of hospitals in Alaska")

[Providence Alaska Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Providence_Alaska_Medical_Center "Providence Alaska Medical Center") in Anchorage is the largest hospital in the state as of 2021;[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-160) Anchorage also hosts [Alaska Regional Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Regional_Hospital "Alaska Regional Hospital") and [Alaska Native Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_Medical_Center "Alaska Native Medical Center").

Alaska's other major cities such as Fairbanks and Juneau also have local hospitals.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-161) In Southeast Alaska, [Southeast Alaska Regional Health Consortium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Alaska_Regional_Health_Consortium "Southeast Alaska Regional Health Consortium"), runs healthcare facilities across 27 communities as of 2022, including hospitals in Sitka and Wrangell;[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-162) although it originally served Native Americans only, it has expanded access and combined with other local facilities over time.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-163)[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-164)

## Education

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=51 "Edit section: Education")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Kachcampus.jpg/220px-Kachcampus.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kachcampus.jpg)

The [Kachemak Bay Campus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kachemak_Bay_Campus "Kachemak Bay Campus") of the [University of Alaska Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alaska_Anchorage "University of Alaska Anchorage"), located in downtown Homer

The [Alaska Department of Education and Early Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Department_of_Education_and_Early_Development "Alaska Department of Education and Early Development") administers many [school districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_school_districts_in_Alaska "List of school districts in Alaska") in Alaska. In addition, the state operates a boarding school, [Mt. Edgecumbe High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mt._Edgecumbe_High_School "Mt. Edgecumbe High School") in [Sitka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitka,_Alaska "Sitka, Alaska"), and provides partial funding for other boarding schools, including [Nenana Student Living Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nenana_Student_Living_Center "Nenana Student Living Center") in [Nenana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nenana,_Alaska "Nenana, Alaska") and The Galena Interior Learning Academy in [Galena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galena,_Alaska "Galena, Alaska").[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-165)

There are more than a dozen [colleges and universities in Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Alaska "List of colleges and universities in Alaska"). Accredited universities in Alaska include the [University of Alaska Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alaska_Anchorage "University of Alaska Anchorage"), [University of Alaska Fairbanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alaska_Fairbanks "University of Alaska Fairbanks"), [University of Alaska Southeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alaska_Southeast "University of Alaska Southeast"), and [Alaska Pacific University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Pacific_University "Alaska Pacific University").[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-166) Alaska is the only state that has no collegiate athletic programs that are members of [NCAA Division I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I "NCAA Division I"), although both Alaska-Fairbanks and Alaska-Anchorage maintain single sport membership in Division I for [men's ice hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_ice_hockey "College ice hockey").

The Alaska Department of Labor and Workforce Development operates AVTEC, Alaska's Institute of Technology.[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-167) Campuses in Seward and Anchorage offer one-week to 11-month training programs in areas as diverse as Information Technology, Welding, Nursing, and Mechanics.

Alaska has had a problem with a "[brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_drain "Brain drain")". Many of its young people, including most of the highest academic achievers, leave the state after high school graduation and do not return. As of 2013[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit), Alaska did not have a [law school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_education_in_Alaska "Legal education in Alaska") or medical school.[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-168) The [University of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alaska "University of Alaska") has attempted to combat this by offering partial four-year scholarships to the top 10% of Alaska high school graduates, via the Alaska Scholars Program.[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-169)

Beginning in 1998, schools in rural Alaska must have at least 10 students to retain funding from the state, and campuses not meeting the number close. This was due to the loss in oil revenues that previously propped up smaller rural schools.[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-170) In 2015, there was a proposal to raise that minimum to 25,[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-171) but legislators in the state largely did not agree.[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-172)

## Transportation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=52 "Edit section: Transportation")]

Main article: [Transportation in Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Alaska "Transportation in Alaska")

### Road

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=53 "Edit section: Road")]

See also: [List of Alaska Routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Alaska_Routes "List of Alaska Routes")

Alaska has few road connections compared to the rest of the U.S. The state's road system, covering a relatively small area of the state, linking the central population centers and the [Alaska Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Highway "Alaska Highway"), the principal route out of the state through Canada. The state capital, Juneau, is not accessible by road, with access only being through ferry or flight;[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-173) this has spurred debate over decades about moving the capital to a city on the road system, or building a road connection from [Haines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haines,_Alaska "Haines, Alaska"). The western part of Alaska has no road system connecting the communities with the rest of Alaska.

The [Interstate Highways in Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Highways_in_Alaska "Interstate Highways in Alaska") consists of a total of 1,082 miles (1,741 km). One unique feature of the Alaska Highway system is the [Anton Anderson Memorial Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Anderson_Memorial_Tunnel "Anton Anderson Memorial Tunnel"), an active [Alaska Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Railroad "Alaska Railroad") tunnel recently upgraded to provide a paved roadway link with the isolated community of [Whittier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whittier,_Alaska "Whittier, Alaska") on [Prince William Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_William_Sound "Prince William Sound") to the [Seward Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seward_Highway "Seward Highway") about 50 miles (80 km) southeast of Anchorage at [Portage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portage,_Alaska "Portage, Alaska"). At 2.5 miles (4.0 km), the tunnel was the longest road tunnel in North America until 2007.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-174) The tunnel is the longest combination [road and rail tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_road-rail_tunnels "List of road-rail tunnels") in North America.

Southwest Alaska is largely coastal, sparsely populated, and unconnected to the road system. Access to most communities in Southwest Alaska is primarily by air taxi, although larger towns like Kodiak, Bethel, King Salmon, Dillingham, and Dutch Harbor are accessible by scheduled air service. Additionally, some coastal communities can be reached via the Alaska Marine Highway ferry.[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-175)

  Alaska welcome sign on the [Klondike Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klondike_Highway "Klondike Highway")

### Rail

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=54 "Edit section: Rail")]

Built around 1915, the [Alaska Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Railroad "Alaska Railroad") (ARR) played a key role in the development of Alaska through the 20th century. It links shipping lanes on the North Pacific with [Interior Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Alaska "Interior Alaska") with tracks that run from [Seward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seward,_Alaska "Seward, Alaska") by way of [South Central Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Central_Alaska "South Central Alaska"), passing through [Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchorage,_Alaska "Anchorage, Alaska"), [Eklutna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eklutna "Eklutna"), [Wasilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wasilla,_Alaska "Wasilla, Alaska"), [Talkeetna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talkeetna,_Alaska "Talkeetna, Alaska"), [Denali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denali "Denali"), and [Fairbanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairbanks,_Alaska "Fairbanks, Alaska"), with spurs to [Whittier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whittier,_Alaska "Whittier, Alaska"), [Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmer,_Alaska "Palmer, Alaska") and [North Pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Pole,_Alaska "North Pole, Alaska"). The cities, towns, villages, and region served by ARR tracks are known statewide as "The Railbelt". In recent years, the ever-improving paved highway system began to eclipse the railroad's importance in Alaska's economy.

The railroad played a vital role in Alaska's development, moving freight into Alaska while transporting natural resources southward, such as coal from the Usibelli coal mine near [Healy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healy,_Alaska "Healy, Alaska") to Seward and gravel from the [Matanuska Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matanuska_Valley "Matanuska Valley") to Anchorage. It is well known for its summertime tour passenger service.

The Alaska Railroad was one of the last railroads in North America to use [cabooses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caboose "Caboose") in regular service and still uses them on some gravel trains. It continues to offer one of the last [flag stop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Request_stop "Request stop") routes in the country. A stretch of about 60 miles (100 km) of track along an area north of Talkeetna remains inaccessible by road; the railroad provides the only transportation to rural homes and cabins in the area. Until construction of the Parks Highway in the 1970s, the railroad provided the only land access to most of the region along its entire route.

In northern Southeast Alaska, the [White Pass and Yukon Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Pass_and_Yukon_Route "White Pass and Yukon Route") also partly runs through the state from [Skagway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skagway,_Alaska "Skagway, Alaska") northwards into Canada ([British Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Columbia "British Columbia") and [Yukon Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yukon_Territory "Yukon Territory")), crossing the border at [White Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Pass "White Pass") Summit. This line is now mainly used by tourists, often arriving by cruise liner at Skagway. It was featured in the 1983 [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") television series *[Great Little Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Little_Railways "Great Little Railways").*

These two railroads are connected neither to each other nor any other railroad. The nearest link to the North American railway network is the northwest terminus of the [Canadian National Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_National_Railway "Canadian National Railway") at [Prince Rupert, British Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Rupert,_British_Columbia "Prince Rupert, British Columbia"), several hundred miles to the southeast. In 2000, the U.S. Congress authorized $6 million to study the feasibility of a rail link between Alaska, Canada, and the [lower 48](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_48 "Lower 48").[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RailLink1-176)[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RailLink2-177)[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RailLink3-178) As of 2021, the [Alaska-Alberta Railway Development Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska-Alberta_Railway_Development_Corporation "Alaska-Alberta Railway Development Corporation") had been placed into receivership.

Some private companies provides [car float](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car_float "Car float") service between [Whittier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whittier,_Alaska "Whittier, Alaska") and [Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle "Seattle").
