  The [White Pass and Yukon Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Pass_and_Yukon_Route "White Pass and Yukon Route") traverses rugged terrain north of [Skagway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skagway "Skagway") near the [Canada–US border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_border "Canada–United States border").

### Sea

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=55 "Edit section: Sea")]

Many cities, towns, and villages in the state do not have road or highway access; the only modes of access involve travel by air, river, or the sea.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Tustumena%2C_Alaska_Marine_Highway.jpg/220px-Tustumena%2C_Alaska_Marine_Highway.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tustumena,_Alaska_Marine_Highway.jpg)

The [MV *Tustumena*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MV_Tustumena "MV Tustumena") (named after [Tustumena Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tustumena_Glacier "Tustumena Glacier")) is one of the state's many ferries, providing service between the [Kenai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenai_Peninsula "Kenai Peninsula"), [Kodiak Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kodiak_Island "Kodiak Island") and the [Aleutian Chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleutian_Chain "Aleutian Chain").

Alaska's well-developed state-owned ferry system (known as the [Alaska Marine Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Marine_Highway "Alaska Marine Highway")) serves the cities of [southeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Alaska "Southeast Alaska"), the Gulf Coast and the Alaska Peninsula. The ferries transport vehicles as well as passengers. The system also operates a ferry service from [Bellingham, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellingham,_Washington "Bellingham, Washington") and [Prince Rupert, British Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Rupert,_British_Columbia "Prince Rupert, British Columbia"), in Canada through the [Inside Passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inside_Passage "Inside Passage") to [Skagway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skagway,_Alaska "Skagway, Alaska"). The [Inter-Island Ferry Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Island_Ferry_Authority "Inter-Island Ferry Authority") also serves as an important marine link for many communities in the [Prince of Wales Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_of_Wales_Island_\(Alaska\) "Prince of Wales Island (Alaska)") region of Southeast and works in concert with the Alaska Marine Highway.

In recent years, cruise lines have created a summertime tourism market, mainly connecting the Pacific Northwest to Southeast Alaska and, to a lesser degree, towns along Alaska's gulf coast. The population of [Ketchikan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketchikan,_Alaska "Ketchikan, Alaska") for example fluctuates dramatically on many days—up to four large cruise ships can dock there at the same time.

### Air

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=56 "Edit section: Air")]

Cities not served by road, sea, or river can be reached only by air, foot, [dogsled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogsled "Dogsled"), or [snowmachine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuous_track#Snow_vehicles "Continuous track"), accounting for Alaska's extremely well developed [bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaskan_Bush "Alaskan Bush") air services—an Alaskan novelty. Anchorage, and to a lesser extent Fairbanks, is served by [many major airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Stevens_Anchorage_International_Airport#Airlines_and_destinations "Ted Stevens Anchorage International Airport"). Because of limited highway access, air travel remains the most efficient form of transportation in and out of the state. Anchorage recently completed extensive remodeling and construction at [Ted Stevens Anchorage International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Stevens_Anchorage_International_Airport "Ted Stevens Anchorage International Airport") to help accommodate the upsurge in tourism (in 2012–2013, Alaska received almost two million visitors).[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-179)

Making regular flights to most villages and towns within the state commercially viable is difficult, so they are heavily subsidized by the federal government through the [Essential Air Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_Air_Service "Essential Air Service") program. [Alaska Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Airlines "Alaska Airlines") is the only major airline offering in-state travel with jet service (sometimes in combination cargo and passenger [Boeing 737](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_737 "Boeing 737")-400s) from Anchorage and [Fairbanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairbanks_International_Airport "Fairbanks International Airport") to regional hubs like [Bethel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethel,_Alaska "Bethel, Alaska"), [Nome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nome,_Alaska "Nome, Alaska"), [Kotzebue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kotzebue,_Alaska "Kotzebue, Alaska"), [Dillingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dillingham,_Alaska "Dillingham, Alaska"), [Kodiak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kodiak,_Alaska "Kodiak, Alaska"), and other larger communities as well as to major Southeast and Alaska Peninsula communities.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/ERA_Aviation_prop_plane_landing_at_ANC_%286194226738%29.jpg/220px-ERA_Aviation_prop_plane_landing_at_ANC_%286194226738%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ERA_Aviation_prop_plane_landing_at_ANC_\(6194226738\).jpg)

A [Bombardier Dash 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombardier_Dash_8 "Bombardier Dash 8"), operated by [Era Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_Alaska "Era Alaska"), on approach to [Ted Stevens Anchorage International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Stevens_Anchorage_International_Airport "Ted Stevens Anchorage International Airport")

The bulk of remaining commercial flight offerings come from small regional commuter airlines such as [Ravn Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravn_Alaska "Ravn Alaska"), [PenAir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PenAir "PenAir"), and [Frontier Flying Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontier_Flying_Service "Frontier Flying Service"). The smallest towns and villages must rely on scheduled or chartered bush flying services using general aviation aircraft such as the [Cessna Caravan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cessna_Caravan "Cessna Caravan"), the most popular aircraft in use in the state. Much of this service can be attributed to the Alaska bypass mail program which subsidizes bulk mail delivery to Alaskan rural communities. The program requires 70% of that subsidy to go to carriers who offer passenger service to the communities.

Many communities have small air taxi services. These operations originated from the demand for customized transport to remote areas. Perhaps the most quintessentially Alaskan plane is the bush seaplane. The world's busiest seaplane base is [Lake Hood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Hood_Seaplane_Base "Lake Hood Seaplane Base"), located next to Ted Stevens Anchorage International Airport, where flights bound for remote villages without an airstrip carry passengers, cargo, and many items from stores and warehouse clubs.

In 2006, Alaska had the highest number of pilots per capita of any U.S. state.[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-180) In Alaska there are 8,795 active pilot certificates as of 2020.[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-181)

### Snow

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=57 "Edit section: Snow")]

Another Alaskan transportation method is the [dogsled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogsled "Dogsled"). In modern times (that is, any time after the mid-late 1920s), dog [mushing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mushing "Mushing") is more of a sport than a true means of transportation. Various races are held around the state, but the best known is the [Iditarod Trail Sled Dog Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iditarod_Trail_Sled_Dog_Race "Iditarod Trail Sled Dog Race"), a 1,150-mile (1,850 km) trail from Anchorage to Nome (although the distance varies from year to year, the official distance is set at 1,049 miles or 1,688 km). The race commemorates the famous [1925 serum run to Nome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1925_serum_run_to_Nome "1925 serum run to Nome") in which mushers and dogs like [Togo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Togo_\(dog\) "Togo (dog)") and [Balto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balto "Balto") took much-needed medicine to the [diphtheria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diphtheria "Diphtheria")-stricken community of [Nome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nome,_Alaska "Nome, Alaska") when all other means of transportation had failed. Mushers from all over the world come to Anchorage each March to compete for cash, prizes, and prestige. The "Serum Run" is another sled dog race that more accurately follows the route of the famous 1925 relay, leaving from the community of [Nenana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nenana,_Alaska "Nenana, Alaska") (southwest of Fairbanks) to Nome.[\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-182)

In areas not served by road or rail, primary transportation in summer is by [all-terrain vehicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-terrain_vehicle "All-terrain vehicle") and in winter by [snowmobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowmobile "Snowmobile") or "snow machine", as it is commonly referred to in Alaska.[\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-183)

## Communication

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=58 "Edit section: Communication")]

Alaska's internet and other data transport systems are provided largely through the two major telecommunications companies: [GCI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GCI_\(company\) "GCI (company)") and [Alaska Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Communications "Alaska Communications"). GCI owns and operates what it calls the Alaska United Fiber Optic system[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-184) and, as of late 2011, Alaska Communications advertised that it has "two fiber optic paths to the lower 48 and two more across Alaska.[\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-185) In January 2011, it was reported that a $1 billion project to connect Asia and rural Alaska was being planned, aided in part by $350 million in stimulus from the federal government.[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-186)

## Law and government

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=59 "Edit section: Law and government")]

### State government

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=60 "Edit section: State government")]

Main article: [Government of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Alaska "Government of Alaska")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Juneau%2C_Alaska_Downtown.jpg/220px-Juneau%2C_Alaska_Downtown.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Juneau,_Alaska_Downtown.jpg)

The center of state government in [Juneau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneau,_Alaska "Juneau, Alaska"). The large buildings in the background are, from left to right: the Court Plaza Building (known colloquially as the "[Spam Can](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spam_\(food\) "Spam (food)")"), the State Office Building (behind), the Alaska Office Building, the [John H. Dimond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_justices_of_the_Alaska_Supreme_Court "List of justices of the Alaska Supreme Court") State Courthouse, and the [Alaska State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_State_Capitol "Alaska State Capitol"). Many of the smaller buildings in the foreground are also occupied by state government agencies.

Like all other U.S. states, Alaska is governed as a republic, with three [branches of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers "Separation of powers"): an [executive branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_branch "Executive branch") consisting of the [governor of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Alaska "Governor of Alaska") and their appointees which head executive departments; a [legislative branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_branch "Legislative branch") consisting of the [Alaska House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_House_of_Representatives "Alaska House of Representatives") and [Alaska Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Senate "Alaska Senate"); and a [judicial branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_branch "Judicial branch") consisting of the [Alaska Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Supreme_Court "Alaska Supreme Court") and lower courts.

The state of Alaska employs approximately 16,000 people statewide.[\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-187)

The [Alaska State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_State_Legislature "Alaska State Legislature") consists of a 40-member [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_House_of_Representatives "Alaska House of Representatives") and a 20-member [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Senate "Alaska Senate"). Senators serve four-year terms and House members two. The [governor of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Alaska "Governor of Alaska") serves four-year terms. The [lieutenant governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Lieutenant_Governors_of_Alaska "List of Lieutenant Governors of Alaska") runs separately from the governor in the [primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-round_system "Two-round system"), but during the general election, the nominee for governor and nominee for lieutenant governor run together on the same ticket.

Alaska's court system has four levels: the [Alaska Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Supreme_Court "Alaska Supreme Court"), the [Alaska Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Court_of_Appeals "Alaska Court of Appeals"), the superior courts and the district courts.[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cts-188) The superior and district courts are [trial courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_court "Trial court"). Superior courts are courts of general jurisdiction, while district courts hear only certain types of cases, including misdemeanor criminal cases and civil cases valued up to $100,000.[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cts-188)

The Supreme Court and the Court of Appeals are [appellate courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appellate_court "Appellate court"). The Court of Appeals is required to hear appeals from certain lower-court decisions, including those regarding criminal prosecutions, juvenile delinquency, and [habeas corpus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habeas_corpus "Habeas corpus").[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cts-188) The Supreme Court hears civil appeals and may in its discretion hear criminal appeals.[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cts-188)

| [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Alaska_gubernatorial_election "2018 Alaska gubernatorial election") | 44.4% *125,739*                                                                                     | **51.4%** *145,631*                                                                                 |                                                                          |
| [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Alaska_gubernatorial_election "2022 Alaska gubernatorial election") | 24.2% *63,755*                                                                                      | **50.3%** *132,392*                                                                                 |                                                                          |

Although in its early years of statehood Alaska was a [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(United_States\) "Democratic Party (United States)") state, since the early 1970s it has been characterized as [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party (United States)")-leaning.[\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-192) Local political communities have often worked on issues related to land use development, fishing, tourism, and individual rights. [Alaska Natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Natives "Alaska Natives"), while organized in and around their communities, have been active within the [Native corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_Regional_Corporations "Alaska Native Regional Corporations"). These have been given ownership over large tracts of land, which require stewardship.

Alaska was formerly the only state in which possession of one ounce or less of marijuana in one's home was completely legal under state law, though the federal law remains in force.[\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-seattle_times-193)

The state has an independence movement favoring a vote on secession from the United States, with the [Alaskan Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaskan_Independence_Party "Alaskan Independence Party").[\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AIPqa-194)

Six [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party (United States)") and four [Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(United_States\) "Democratic Party (United States)") have served as governor of Alaska. In addition, Republican governor [Wally Hickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wally_Hickel "Wally Hickel") was elected to the office for a second term in 1990 after leaving the Republican party and briefly joining the Alaskan Independence Party ticket just long enough to be reelected. He officially rejoined the Republican party in 1994.

Alaska's [voter initiative making marijuana legal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Alaska_Measure_2 "2014 Alaska Measure 2") took effect on February 24, 2015, placing Alaska alongside Colorado and Washington, as well as Washington D.C., as the first three U.S. states where recreational marijuana is legal. The new law means people over 21 can consume small amounts of cannabis.[\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-195) The first legal marijuana store opened in Valdez in October 2016.[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-196)

### Taxes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=63 "Edit section: Taxes")]

To finance state government operations, Alaska depends primarily on petroleum revenues and federal subsidies. This allows it to have the lowest individual tax burden in the United States.[\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-198) It is one of five states with no [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax "Sales tax"), one of seven states with no individual [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax "Income tax"), and—along with [New Hampshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Hampshire "New Hampshire")—one of two that has neither.[\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-199) The Department of Revenue Tax Division[\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-200) reports regularly on the state's revenue sources. The department also issues an annual summary of its operations, including new state laws that directly affect the tax division. In 2014, the [Tax Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Foundation "Tax Foundation") ranked Alaska as having the fourth most "business friendly" tax policy, behind only [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming "Wyoming"), [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota "South Dakota"), and [Nevada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevada "Nevada").[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-201)

While Alaska has no state sales tax, 89 municipalities collect a local sales tax, from 1.0 to 7.5%, typically 3–5%. Other local taxes levied include raw fish taxes, hotel, motel, and bed-and-breakfast 'bed' taxes, [severance taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severance_tax "Severance tax"), liquor and tobacco taxes, gaming (pull tabs) taxes, tire taxes and fuel transfer taxes. A part of the revenue collected from certain state taxes and license fees (such as petroleum, aviation motor fuel, telephone cooperative) is shared with municipalities in Alaska.

The fall in oil prices after the [fracking boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydraulic_fracturing_in_the_United_States "Hydraulic fracturing in the United States") in the early 2010s has decimated Alaska's state treasury, which has historically received about 85 percent of its revenue from taxes and fees imposed on oil and gas companies.[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-202) The state government has had to drastically reduce its budget, and has brought its budget shortfall from over $2 billion in 2016 to under $500 million by 2018. In 2020, Alaska's state government budget was $4.8 billion, while projected government revenues were only $4.5 billion.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-203)

### Federal politics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=64 "Edit section: Federal politics")]

Main article: [Politics of Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Alaska "Politics of Alaska")

See also: [Arctic Policy of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Policy_of_the_United_States "Arctic Policy of the United States")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Presidential_Vote_in_Alaska%2C_1960-2020.svg/220px-Presidential_Vote_in_Alaska%2C_1960-2020.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Presidential_Vote_in_Alaska,_1960-2020.svg)

A line graph showing the presidential vote by party from 1960 to 2020 in Alaska

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Don_Young%2C_official_115th_Congress_photo_portrait.jpg/220px-Don_Young%2C_official_115th_Congress_photo_portrait.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Don_Young,_official_115th_Congress_photo_portrait.jpg)

Republican [Don Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Young "Don Young") held Alaska's sole U.S. House seat for 49 years, from 1973 to 2022.

Alaska regularly supports [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party (United States)") in presidential elections and has done so since statehood. Republicans have won the state's [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_College_\(United_States\) "Electoral College (United States)") votes in all but one election that it has participated in ([1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election "1964 United States presidential election")). No state has voted for a [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(United_States\) "Democratic Party (United States)") presidential candidate fewer times. Alaska was carried by Democratic nominee [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson "Lyndon B. Johnson") during his landslide election in [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election "1964 United States presidential election"), while the [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_United_States_presidential_election "1960 United States presidential election") and [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_United_States_presidential_election "1968 United States presidential election") elections were close. Since [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_presidential_election "1972 United States presidential election"), Republicans have carried the state by large margins. In [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election "2008 United States presidential election"), Republican [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain "John McCain") defeated Democrat [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama") in Alaska, 59.49% to 37.83%. McCain's running mate was [Sarah Palin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Palin "Sarah Palin"), the state's governor and the first Alaskan on a major party ticket. Obama lost Alaska again in [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election "2012 United States presidential election"), but he captured 40% of the state's vote in that election, making him the first Democrat to do so since 1968. In [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election "2020 United States presidential election"), [Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden "Joe Biden") received 42.77% of the vote for president, marking the high point for a Democratic presidential candidate since Johnson's 1964 victory.

The [Alaska Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bush_\(Alaska\) "The Bush (Alaska)"), central Juneau, midtown and downtown Anchorage, and the areas surrounding the [University of Alaska Fairbanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alaska_Fairbanks "University of Alaska Fairbanks") campus and Ester have been strongholds of the Democratic Party. The Matanuska-Susitna Borough, the majority of Fairbanks (including North Pole and the military base), and South Anchorage typically have the strongest Republican showing.

### Elections

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=65 "Edit section: Elections")]

See also: [Ranked-choice voting in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranked-choice_voting_in_the_United_States "Ranked-choice voting in the United States") and [Nonpartisan blanket primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonpartisan_blanket_primary "Nonpartisan blanket primary")

Alaska has a history of primary defeats for incumbent U.S. Senators, including Ernest Gruening, Mike Gravel, and Lisa Murkowski. However, Murkowski won re-election with a write-in campaign. Despite this, Alaska has also seen long-serving members of Congress, such as Ted Stevens, who served as a U.S. Senator for 40 years, and Don Young, who held Alaska's sole U.S. House seat for 49 years (from 1973 to 2022).

In the [2020 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Alaska_elections "2020 Alaska elections") cycle, Alaskan voters approved Ballot Measure 2.[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-204) The measure passed by a margin of 1.1%, or about 4,000 votes.[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0b-205) The measure requires campaigns to disclose the original source and any intermediaries for campaign contributions over $2,000. The measure also establishes [non-partisan blanket primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonpartisan_blanket_primary "Nonpartisan blanket primary") for statewide elections (like in [Washington state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_\(state\)#Elections "Washington (state)") and [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California#Government_and_politics "California")) and [ranked-choice voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instant-runoff_voting "Instant-runoff voting") (like in [Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine#Politics "Maine")).[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0b-205) Measure 2 makes Alaska the third state with [jungle primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jungle_primary "Jungle primary") for all statewide races, the second state with [ranked choice voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranked_voting "Ranked voting"), and the only state with both.

The 2022 special election to fill Alaska's only U.S. House seat, left vacant by the death of Don Young, was won by Mary Peltola. She became the first Democrat to win the House seat since 1972 and the first Alaskan Native elected to the United States Congress in history. After winning a full term in the 2022 general election, Peltola lost reelection in 2024 to Republican Nick Begich III.

## Notes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alaska\&action=edit\&section=67 "Edit section: Notes")]

1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-25)** These three Aleutian outer islands are about 460 miles (740 km) away from mainland USSR, 920 miles (1,480 km) from mainland Alaska, 950 miles (1,530 km) from Japan.
2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-190)** Wally Hickel would rejoin the Republican party after winning the election as a member of the Alaskan Independence Party.
3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-191)** [Byron Mallott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byron_Mallott "Byron Mallott"), the Democratic gubernatorial nominee, suspended his campaign and became the running mate of [Bill Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Walker_\(American_politician\) "Bill Walker (American politician)"), an independent who left the Republican Party. They won the election with 48.1% or 134,658 votes.
